I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n days and need to ensure that for any two consecutive days, the sum of walks on those days is at least k.
2. We start with planned walks `a_i` for each day, but we may need to add more walks.
3. The days before day 1 and after day n are assumed to have exactly k walks.
4. We need to find the minimum additional walks needed and the resulting schedule.

## Key Insight

For any two consecutive days i and i+1, we need: `b_i + b_{i+1} >= k`

Since we want to minimize additional walks, we should only increase the walks when necessary.

## Strategy

1. We need to ensure that for each consecutive pair (i, i+1): `b_i + b_{i+1} >= k`
2. Since we already have `a_i` walks on day i, we can calculate how many more walks we need for each day.
3. The constraint applies to pairs: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n)
4. We also have external constraints: day 0 has k walks and day n+1 has k walks.

Wait, let me reconsider the problem statement. It says:
- "on the day before the first day and on the day after the n-th day Polycarp will go for a walk with Cormen exactly k times"

This means:
- The sum of walks on day 0 (before day 1) and day 1 should be ≥ k, so `a_1 + k ≥ k` (always true)
- The sum of walks on day 1 and day 2 should be ≥ k
- The sum of walks on day 2 and day 3 should be ≥ k
- ...
- The sum of walks on day n-1 and day n should be ≥ k
- The sum of walks on day n and day n+1 (after day n) should be ≥ k, so `a_n + k ≥ k` (always true)

Actually, the constraint applies to consecutive days in the n-day period, so we need:
- `b_1 + b_2 ≥ k`
- `b_2 + b_3 ≥ k`
- ...
- `b_{n-1} + b_n ≥ k`

And we need to maintain `b_i ≥ a_i`.

## Algorithm

For each day i from 1 to n-1, we need: `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`

We can process this greedily:
1. Start with `b_i = a_i` for all i
2. For each consecutive pair, if `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`, we need to increase one of them
3. To minimize total additional walks, we should increase the one that will help satisfy more constraints

Actually, let's think simpler:
- We need to satisfy all consecutive constraints: `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k` for i from 1 to n-1
- We can solve this by going through the pairs and ensuring each constraint is met

A simpler approach:
- Start with b_i = a_i
- For each i from 1 to n-1:
  - If b_i + b_{i+1} < k, we need to increase the sum
  - We can increase either b_i or b_{i+1}, or both
  - To minimize additional walks, we should increase the one that helps the most
  - Actually, we can increase b_{i+1} because it will help the next constraint too

Wait, let me implement a simpler greedy approach:

1. Initialize b_i = a_i for all i
2. For each i from 1 to n-1:
   - If b_i + b_{i+1} < k, then we need to add (k - (b_i + b_{i+1})) more walks
   - We can add these walks to b_{i+1} to satisfy this constraint
3. This way, we're only making changes when necessary, and we're adding walks in a way that might help future constraints (but we're not making them worse)

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: n=3, k=5, a=[2,0,1]
- Start: b=[2,0,1]
- Pair (1,2): b1+b2 = 2+0 = 2 < 5, need 3 more walks
  - Add 3 to b2: b=[2,3,1] 
- Pair (2,3): b2+b3 = 3+1 = 4 < 5, need 1 more walks  
  - Add 1 to b3: b=[2,3,2]
- Total additional = 3+1 = 4 ✓

Example 2: n=3, k=1, a=[0,0,0]
- Start: b=[0,0,0]
- Pair (1,2): 0+0=0 < 1, need 1 more
  - Add 1 to b2: b=[0,1,0]
- Pair (2,3): 1+0=1 ≥ 1, good
- Total additional = 1 ✓

This matches. So the algorithm works.

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

b = a[:]  # copy of original
additional_walks = 0

# Check each consecutive pair
for i in range(n - 1):
    if b[i] + b[i + 1] < k:
        # Need to add more walks to make sum >= k
        needed = k - (b[i] + b[i + 1])
        additional_walks += needed
        b[i + 1] += needed  # add to the next day to help future constraints

print(additional_walks)
print(*b)
```

My algorithm above adds to the next day, which might affect future constraints correctly.

Let me verify with the third example:

- Start: b=[2,4,3,5]
- Pair (1,2): 2+4=6 ≥ 6 ✓
- Pair (2,3): 4+3=7 ≥ 6 ✓  
- Pair (3,4): 3+5=8 ≥ 6 ✓
- No changes needed, additional = 0 ✓

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Recently a dog was bought for Polycarp. The dog's name is Cormen. Now Polycarp has a lot of troubles. For example, Cormen likes going for a walk. 

Empirically Polycarp learned that the dog needs at least k walks for any two consecutive days in order to feel good. For example, if k = 5 and yesterday Polycarp went for a walk with Cormen 2 times, today he has to go for a walk at least 3 times. 

Polycarp analysed all his affairs over the next n days and made a sequence of n integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n}, where a_{i} is the number of times Polycarp will walk with the dog on the i-th day while doing all his affairs (for example, he has to go to a shop, throw out the trash, etc.).

Help Polycarp determine the minimum number of walks he needs to do additionaly in the next n days so that Cormen will feel good during all the n days. You can assume that on the day before the first day and on the day after the n-th day Polycarp will go for a walk with Cormen exactly k times. 

Write a program that will find the minumum number of additional walks and the appropriate schedule — the sequence of integers b_1, b_2, ..., b_{n} (b_{i} ≥ a_{i}), where b_{i} means the total number of walks with the dog on the i-th day.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers n and k (1 ≤ n, k ≤ 500) — the number of days and the minimum number of walks with Cormen for any two consecutive days. 

The second line contains integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (0 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 500) — the number of walks with Cormen on the i-th day which Polycarp has already planned. 


-----Output-----

In the first line print the smallest number of additional walks that Polycarp should do during the next n days so that Cormen will feel good during all days. 

In the second line print n integers b_1, b_2, ..., b_{n}, where b_{i} — the total number of walks on the i-th day according to the found solutions (a_{i} ≤ b_{i} for all i from 1 to n). If there are multiple solutions, print any of them. 


-----Examples-----
Input
3 5
2 0 1

Output
4
2 3 2

Input
3 1
0 0 0

Output
1
0 1 0

Input
4 6
2 4 3 5

Output
0
2 4 3 5

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days and a dog that needs at least `k` walks for any two consecutive days
2. We have a planned sequence `a_1, a_2, ..., a_n` of walks per day
3. On days before day 1 and after day n, there are exactly `k` walks
4. We need to find the minimum additional walks such that for every consecutive pair of days `(i, i+1)`, the total walks are at least `k`
5. We need to output the total walks `b_i` on each day, where `b_i >= a_i`

## Key Insight

For each consecutive pair of days `(i, i+1)`, we need `b_i + b_{i+1} >= k`.

Since we want to minimize additional walks, we should only increase the values when necessary.

## Strategy

For each day `i` (from 1 to n-1), we need to ensure `b_i + b_{i+1} >= k`.

The constraint is: `b_i + b_{i+1} >= k` for all `i` from 1 to n-1.

Given that `b_i >= a_i`, we can think about this as a constraint satisfaction problem where we need to satisfy all adjacent constraints with minimal increases.

### Approach:
1. Start with the initial plan `a_i`
2. For each adjacent pair `(i, i+1)`, if `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, we need to add more walks
3. We can distribute the additional walks between the two days to minimize the total increase

However, there's a subtlety: we need to consider that each day might be involved in multiple constraints.

Actually, let me think about this more systematically:

For each constraint `b_i + b_{i+1} >= k`:
- If `a_i + a_{i+1} >= k`, no additional walks needed for this pair
- If `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, we need to add at least `k - (a_i + a_{i+1})` walks in total between days `i` and `i+1`

To minimize the total additional walks, we should distribute the deficit optimally.

1. Start with `b_i = a_i` for all i

3. We can add the deficit to either day `i` or day `i+1`, but we need to consider future constraints as well

Actually, since we want to minimize total additional walks, we can use a greedy approach that processes constraints and adds the minimum required.

However, the optimal solution is simpler: for each pair `(i, i+1)`, add the required deficit to the day that will have fewer constraints affecting it in the future.

But since the constraints are only between consecutive days, and we're looking for minimum additional walks, we can simply:

1. For each adjacent pair `(i, i+1)`, if `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, we need to add `k - (a_i + a_{i+1})` walks in total between these two days
2. We can add these walks optimally to either day `i` or day `i+1` to minimize the overall impact

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we need to ensure that for each pair `(i, i+1)`, `b_i + b_{i+1} >= k`.

The key insight is that we can process the constraints sequentially and greedily add the minimum needed.

Let me implement a simple solution:

1. Initialize `b_i = a_i` for all i
2. For each pair `(i, i+1)` from 1 to n-1:
   - If `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`, we need to add `k - (b_i + b_{i+1})` walks
   - We can add these walks to either `b_i` or `b_{i+1}`, but to minimize future issues, we might want to add them to the day that's not yet constrained

Actually, since all constraints are independent and we just need to satisfy each adjacent pair, we can add the deficit to the first day in the pair, or we can add it to the second day.

The minimum total additional walks will be achieved by optimally distributing the deficits.

But here's a simpler observation: we don't need to be too smart about distribution. Any valid distribution that satisfies the constraints will work, and we want to minimize the total additional walks.

Since the constraints are only between adjacent days, and we're adding non-negative values, we can just greedily add the deficit to one of the days.

Let me implement a simple greedy approach: for each pair `(i, i+1)` where `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, add the deficit to `b_{i+1}` (the later day).

This way, we ensure that the constraint for pair `(i, i+1)` is satisfied, and we don't over-constrain earlier days.

Actually, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=3, k=5, a=[2,0,1]
- Pair (1,2): 2+0=2 < 5, need to add 3 more
- Pair (2,3): 0+1=1 < 5, need to add 4 more

So I need to add 1 to b3: b3 becomes 1+1=2

Total additional: 3+1=4, which matches expected output [2,3,2]

This seems correct.

Algorithm:
1. Initialize b_i = a_i for all i
2. For each day i from 0 to n-2 (0-indexed):
   - If b_i + b_{i+1} < k:
     - deficit = k - (b_i + b_{i+1})
     - We can add this deficit to either b_i or b_{i+1}
     - To avoid over-constraining future pairs, we should add it to b_{i+1} (the later day)
     - So add deficit to b_{i+1}
3. Return the result

Let me check example 2: n=3, k=1, a=[0,0,0]
- Pair (0,1): 0+0=0 < 1, deficit=1, add to b1 -> b1=1
- Pair (1,2): 1+0=1 >= 1, no change
- Result: [0,1,0], additional walks = 1, matches expected

Example 3: n=4, k=6, a=[2,4,3,5]
- Pair (0,1): 2+4=6 >= 6, no change
- Pair (1,2): 4+3=7 >= 6, no change  
- Pair (2,3): 3+5=8 >= 6, no change
- Result: [2,4,3,5], additional walks = 0, matches expected

Perfect! The greedy approach works.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Initialize b with a
b = a[:]

# Process each consecutive pair
for i in range(n - 1):
    if b[i] + b[i + 1] < k:
        deficit = k - (b[i] + b[i + 1])
        # Add the deficit to the later day (b[i+1]) to avoid affecting earlier constraints
        b[i + 1] += deficit

# Calculate total additional walks
additional_walks = sum(b[i] - a[i] for i in range(n))

print(additional_walks)
print(*b)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days and a dog that needs at least `k` walks for any two consecutive days
2. On day `i`, we have a planned number of walks `a_i`
3. We need to find the minimum additional walks to add so that for any two consecutive days, the total walks ≥ `k`
4. The days before day 1 and after day n have exactly `k` walks (boundary conditions)

## Key Insights

1. For consecutive days `i` and `i+1`, we need `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`
2. Since we want to minimize additional walks, we start with the original plan `a_i` and only increase when necessary
3. We need to ensure that for every pair of consecutive days, the sum is at least `k`

## Strategy

1. For each day `i`, we need to check if `a_i + a_{i+1} ≥ k`
2. If not, we need to increase one or both of them
3. To minimize total additional walks, we should consider the constraint from both directions

Let me think of a greedy approach:
- For each day `i`, calculate the minimum additional walks needed
- For day `i`, the constraint with day `i-1` and day `i+1` both must be satisfied
- We can process the array from left to right and ensure each consecutive pair satisfies the constraint

Actually, let me use a simpler approach:
1. Start with `b_i = a_i` for all days
2. Check each consecutive pair `(i, i+1)` 
3. If `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`, we need to increase either `b_i` or `b_{i+1}` (or both)
4. To minimize total additional walks, we should increase the one that will help with future constraints

But there's a better approach: we can process from left to right and when a constraint is violated, we increase the current day.

Wait, let me reconsider with the boundary conditions:
- Day 0 (before day 1): has `k` walks
- Day n+1 (after day n): has `k` walks

So for the first day, we need `b_1 + k ≥ k` which is always true since `k ≥ 0`.
For the last day, we need `b_n + k ≥ k` which is always true.

The constraints are only between consecutive days in the array: `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k` for all `i` from 1 to `n-1`.

## Algorithm

1. Initialize `b_i = a_i` for all `i`
2. For each day `i` from 1 to `n-1`, check if `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`
3. If it is, we need to increase one of them. To minimize total additional walks, we can increase `b_{i+1}` by the difference, or increase `b_i` by the difference.
4. Actually, we can increase `b_{i+1}` since it will also help with the constraint between `i+1` and `i+2` in the next iteration.

But to be optimal, let's consider: if `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`, we need to add at least `(k - (b_i + b_{i+1}))` walks in total.

The optimal strategy is to increase `b_{i+1}` by `k - (b_i + b_{i+1})`, because:
- It fixes the constraint between day `i` and `i+1`
- It doesn't affect the constraint between day `i-1` and `i` (since we only check day `i` with day `i+1`)
- It might be better than increasing `b_i` since `b_i` is already constrained by previous day

Actually, since we process left to right, we can only ensure that the current constraint is satisfied. 

Let me implement a greedy left-to-right approach:

1. Set `b_i = a_i` for all `i`
2. For `i` from 1 to `n-1`:
   - If `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`, then we need to increase `b_{i+1}` by `k - (b_i + b_{i+1})`
3. This ensures all consecutive constraints are satisfied.

Wait, this might not work because we're only looking at the constraint between consecutive days, and we're fixing it by increasing the later day.

Let me verify with the first example:
- n=3, k=5, a=[2,0,1]
- Start: b=[2,0,1]
- Check day 1-2: 2+0=2 < 5, so increase b[2] by 5-2=3 → b=[2,3,1]
- Check day 2-3: 3+1=4 < 5, so increase b[3] by 5-4=1 → b=[2,3,2]

Let me verify with the second example:
- n=3, k=1, a=[0,0,0]
- Start: b=[0,0,0]
- Check day 1-2: 0+0=0 < 1, so increase b[2] by 1 → b=[0,1,0]
- Check day 2-3: 1+0=1 ≥ 1, already satisfied
- Total additional walks: 1 ✓

This also matches.

The algorithm:
1. Read n, k and array a
2. Initialize b = a
3. For i from 0 to n-2:
   - If b[i] + b[i+1] < k:
     - additional = k - (b[i] + b[i+1])
     - b[i+1] += additional
4. Sum up all additional walks
5. Output the total and the array b

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days and need to ensure that for any two consecutive days, the total walks are at least `k`.
2. We have a planned schedule `a_i` for each day, and we can only increase the number of walks (not decrease).
3. The day before day 1 and day after day n both have exactly `k` walks.
4. We need to find the minimum additional walks needed and the final schedule `b_i`.

## Key Insights

1. For day 1: The constraint is that day 0 (before day 1) + day 1 ≥ k. Since day 0 has k walks, we need `k + a_1 ≥ k`, so we actually need `a_1 ≥ 0` which is always true. Wait, let me reconsider.

Actually, the constraint is about consecutive days: for any two consecutive days `i` and `i+1`, we need `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`.

So for each consecutive pair:
- Days 0 and 1: `k + b_1 ≥ k` → `b_1 ≥ 0` (always true)
- Days 1 and 2: `b_1 + b_2 ≥ k`
- Days 2 and 3: `b_2 + b_3 ≥ k`
- ...
- Days n-1 and n: `b_{n-1} + k ≥ k` → `b_{n-1} ≥ 0` (always true)

Wait, I think I misunderstood. The constraint is for any two consecutive days, the sum of walks on those two days should be at least k.

So:
- For days 1 and 2: `b_1 + b_2 ≥ k`
- For days 2 and 3: `b_2 + b_3 ≥ k`
- ...
- For days n-1 and n: `b_{n-1} + b_n ≥ k`

But the problem states: "the dog needs at least k walks for any two consecutive days". This means the sum of walks on any two consecutive days should be at least k.

So we need:
- `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k` for all `i` from 1 to n-1.

## Solution Approach

1. We start with the initial plan `a_i`.
2. For each pair of consecutive days (i, i+1), if `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, we need to increase one or both of them.
3. To minimize total additional walks, we should only increase when necessary, and we can distribute the increase optimally.

The greedy approach:
- Go through each consecutive pair from left to right.
- If `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, we need to add `k - (a_i + a_{i+1})` more walks.
- We can add this to either `a_i` or `a_{i+1}`. To avoid violating previous constraints, we should add to the later day.

Wait, let me think more carefully. If we add to a day, it might help with multiple constraints. 

Actually, if we add to day i, it helps with constraints (i-1, i) and (i, i+1). But since we're processing from left to right, we don't need to worry about previous constraints because we're only checking the current pair.

So algorithm:
1. Start with `b_i = a_i` for all i.
2. For each i from 1 to n-1:
   - If `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`:
     - We need to add `k - (b_i + b_{i+1})` more walks
     - We should add this to `b_{i+1}` to avoid affecting future constraints (since adding to earlier days might require fixing constraints we've already processed)
     - However, adding to `b_{i+1}` might help with constraints (i+1, i+2) later.

Actually, we can just add the deficit to the later day to keep the current constraint satisfied and not break previous ones.

But let's verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=3, k=5, a=[2,0,1]
- Day 1,2: 2+0 = 2 < 5, need 3 more walks → add to day 2: b=[2,3,1]
- Day 2,3: 3+1 = 4 < 5, need 1 more walks → add to day 3: b=[2,3,2]
- Total additional: 3+1 = 4
- This matches the expected output.

Example 2: n=3, k=1, a=[0,0,0]
- Day 1,2: 0+0 = 0 < 1, need 1 more → add to day 2: b=[0,1,0]
- Day 2,3: 1+0 = 1 ≥ 1, no need
- Total additional: 1
- This matches.

So the algorithm is:
1. Initialize `b = a`
2. For i from 0 to n-2:
   - If `b[i] + b[i+1] < k`:
     - diff = k - (b[i] + b[i+1])
     - Add diff to `b[i+1]`
3. Total additional walks is sum of all diffs added.

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

b = a.copy()
additional = 0

for i in range(n - 1):
    if b[i] + b[i + 1] < k:
        diff = k - (b[i] + b[i + 1])
        b[i + 1] += diff
        additional += diff

print(additional)
print(*b)
```

Let me verify with example 3:
n=4, k=6, a=[2,4,3,5]
- Day 1,2: 2+4 = 6 ≥ 6, no change
- Day 2,3: 4+3 = 7 ≥ 6, no change  
- Day 3,4: 3+5 = 8 ≥ 6, no change
- Additional = 0
- Output = [2,4,3,5] ✓

Perfect! The solution works.

Help Polycarp determine the minimum number of walks he needs to do additionaly in the next n days so that Cormen will feel good during all the n days. You can assume that on the day before the first day and on the day after the n-th day Polycarp will go for a walk with Cormen exactly k times. 

Write a program that will find the minumum number of additional walks and the appropriate schedule — the sequence of integers b_1, b_2, ..., b_{n} (b_{i} ≥ a_{i}), where b_{i} means the total number of walks with the dog on the i-th day.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n days and a dog that needs at least k walks for any two consecutive days
2. We have a planned number of walks `a_i` for each day
3. We need to find the minimum number of additional walks to add so that for every pair of consecutive days, the total walks sum to at least k
4. The days before day 1 and after day n have exactly k walks (so we need to consider boundary conditions)

## Key Insights

1. For consecutive days i and i+1, we need: `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`
2. We already have `b_i ≥ a_i`, so we need to ensure that if `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, we need to add more walks
3. The constraint applies from day 1 to day n-1 (consecutive pairs)
4. The days before day 1 and after day n have k walks, but we don't need to worry about those as they're not part of our planned sequence

## Algorithm

1. Start with the original plan `b_i = a_i`
2. For each consecutive pair (i, i+1), check if `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`
3. If yes, we need to add more walks. We can add to either day i or day i+1 (preferably to minimize total additions)
4. To minimize the total additional walks, we should add as few as possible to satisfy each constraint

4. The deficit for pair (i, i+1) is `k - (b[i] + b[i+1])`

Wait, actually we need to think about when we process the constraints. If we process from left to right, adding to the right day only affects future pairs.

But the problem has a constraint that we need to satisfy all consecutive pairs.

Actually, the simplest approach is:
- Start with original values
- For each consecutive pair from left to right, if the sum is less than k, add the deficit to the second day
- This ensures we satisfy all constraints

Let me trace through the examples:

- Pair (1,2): 2+0=2 < 5, need to add 3, add to day 2: b[1]=2, b[2]=0+3=3

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

b = a[:]  # copy of a
additional_walks = 0

    if b[i] + b[i + 1] < k:
        deficit = k - (b[i] + b[i + 1])
        b[i + 1] += deficit
        additional_walks += deficit

print(additional_walks)
print(*b)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days and a dog that needs at least `k` walks for any two consecutive days to feel good.
2. We have a planned schedule `a_i` for each day, but we might need to add more walks.
3. On the day before day 1 and after day n, there are exactly `k` walks.
4. We need to find the minimum number of additional walks to add so that consecutive days both have at least `k` total walks.

## Key Insight

For any two consecutive days i and i+1, we need:
- `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`

Since we start with planned walks `a_i`, we need to ensure that for each pair of consecutive days, the sum is at least `k`.

The constraint is: `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`

We want to minimize the total additional walks, which is `sum(b_i) - sum(a_i)`.

## Strategy

We can use a greedy approach with the following insight:
- For each day i, we have a base value `a_i`
- For each constraint `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`, we need to ensure this holds
- We can work from left to right, ensuring each constraint is met

Actually, let me think about this more systematically:

For each constraint `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`:
- If `a_i + a_{i+1} ≥ k`, we don't need to add any walks for this pair
- If `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, we need to add `k - (a_i + a_{i+1})` walks in total between days i and i+1

But we have to decide how to distribute these additional walks between days i and i+1 to minimize the total additional walks.

Since we want to minimize the total additional walks, and we have multiple constraints, we can process the constraints and adjust the values greedily.

However, there's a simpler observation: we just need to ensure that for each consecutive pair, their sum is at least k. We can do this by going through each pair and making the necessary adjustments.

## Algorithm

1. Start with the original array `a`
2. For each consecutive pair (i, i+1), if `a_i + a_{i+1} < k`, we need to add more walks
3. To minimize total additional walks, we can add the deficit to the later day (or distribute optimally)

But actually, since we want to minimize the total additional walks, we can just add to the second day in each pair where needed, or distribute optimally.

Let me use a simpler approach: go through each pair and ensure the sum is at least k, adding the deficit to the second day of the pair (this ensures we don't create new constraints with the next pair).

Wait, this might not be optimal because adding to one day might affect multiple constraints.

Actually, since each constraint is independent and we just need to satisfy `b_i + b_{i+1} ≥ k`, the minimum additional walks is achieved by simply fixing each constraint where needed.

Let me try a greedy solution:

1. Create array `b` where `b_i = a_i` initially
2. For each pair (i, i+1):
   - If `b_i + b_{i+1} < k`, we need to add `k - (b_i + b_{i+1})` walks
   - Add these walks to `b_{i+1}` (this doesn't create new constraints with earlier days)

This works because:
- We're only fixing the constraint for the current pair
- By adding to the later day, we don't create a new constraint with the previous day (since we're not changing earlier values)
- This ensures all constraints are satisfied

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: n=3, k=5, a=[2,0,1]
- Pair (0,1): 2+0=2 < 5, need 3 more. Add to b[1]: b becomes [2,3,1]
- Pair (1,2): 3+1=4 < 5, need 1 more. Add to b[2]: b becomes [2,3,2]
- Total additional = 3+1 = 4 ✓

Example 2: n=3, k=1, a=[0,0,0]
- Pair (0,1): 0+0=0 < 1, need 1 more. Add to b[1]: b becomes [0,1,0]
- Pair (1,2): 1+0=1 ≥ 1, no change
- Total additional = 1 ✓

This matches!

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

b = a[:]  # copy of a
additional = 0

# For each consecutive pair
for i in range(n - 1):
    if b[i] + b[i + 1] < k:
        # Need to add more walks to make the sum at least k
        need = k - (b[i] + b[i + 1])
        b[i + 1] += need
        additional += need

print(additional)
print(*b)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.


-----Example-----
Input
10 6
1 2
1 3
1 4
2 5
2 6
3 7
7 8
7 9
9 10
4 3 8 9 10
3 2 4 6
3 2 1 5
3 4 8 2
2 6 10
3 5 4 7

Output
YES
YES
YES
YES
NO
NO

Consider the queries.

The first query is $[3, 8, 9, 10]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path from the root $1$ to the vertex $u=10$. Then vertices $[3, 9, 10]$ belong to the path from $1$ to $10$ and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which also belongs to this path.

The second query is $[2, 4, 6]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=2$. Then the vertex $4$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $6$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $2$ which belongs to this path.

The third query is $[2, 1, 5]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=5$ and all vertices of the query belong to this path.

The fourth query is $[4, 8, 2]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=9$ so vertices $2$ and $4$ both have distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which belongs to this path.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a rooted tree with n vertices, root at vertex 1
2. For each query, we're given k distinct vertices
3. We need to determine if there exists a path from root (vertex 1) to some vertex u such that:
   - Each query vertex either:
     - Belongs to the path from root to u, OR
     - Has distance exactly 1 (adjacent) to some vertex on that path

## Key Insights

1. The path from root to u is a simple path from 1 to u
2. For a vertex v to be "covered":
   - Either v is on the path from 1 to u, OR
   - v is adjacent to some vertex on the path from 1 to u
3. Since we're looking for a path from root to some u, all vertices on the path must be in the subtree of u, and we need to check if all query vertices can be covered by this structure.

## Strategy

1. Build the tree from input edges
2. For each query, we need to check if there exists some vertex u such that all query vertices are covered by the path from 1 to u
3. Key observation: The path from 1 to u must contain all the "closest" nodes to the query vertices. Specifically, if a query vertex v is not on the path, then it must be adjacent to some vertex on the path.

4. For a query to be feasible:
   - All query vertices must lie in the same "connected component" when considering the tree structure
   - The path must extend to cover all query vertices either directly or through adjacency

## Algorithm

1. Preprocess the tree to get parent-child relationships and depths
2. For each query:
   - Consider the set of query vertices
   - The path from root to u must cover all vertices in the query either directly or through adjacency
   - A vertex v not on the path must have a neighbor that is on the path
   - The path must extend to the deepest query vertex in terms of depth
   - The path must be such that for each query vertex v, either:
     - v is on the path, or
     - parent(v) or child(v) is on the path

     - candidate_set.add(v)

We'll implement with the candidate set as {v, parent(v) for v in query, child(w) for w in query}.

In code, for each query vertex v, we add v, parent[v], and for each child w of v, we add w.

But to avoid duplicates, we use a set.

Also, we need to handle the case where parent[v] is 0 (not exists), or when v is root.

For root, parent is undefined, so we skip.

Let's implement accordingly.

Steps:
1. Read n, m
2. Build graph and compute parent and children arrays using BFS/DFS from root
3. For each query:
   - candidate_set = set()
   - for each vertex in query:
     - add vertex
     - if vertex is not root (1), add parent[vertex]
     - for each child of vertex in tree:
       - add child
   - For each candidate u in candidate_set:
     - Build path from 1 to u (ancestors of u)
     - Check if every query vertex is covered by path (i.e., v is in path or parent(v) is in path or some child of v is in path)
   - If any candidate works, print "YES", else "NO"

But to build path from 1 to u, we can go from u to root using parent pointers, and store the set of ancestors.

To check if a vertex v is covered by path to u:
- v is in path, or
- parent(v) is in path, or
- any child of v is in path

But "any child of v" means any direct child of v in the tree.

So we need to know the children of v.

We can precompute children of each vertex.

In the implementation, we'll store children as a dictionary or list.

Let's code accordingly.

Note: we need to build the tree first.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def main():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    n = int(data[0])
    m = int(data[1])
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    index = 2
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[index])
        v = int(data[index+1])
        index += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # BFS to build parent and children arrays
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    children = defaultdict(list)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    parent[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                children[u].append(v)
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Process queries
    queries = []
    for i in range(m):
        k = int(data[index])
        index += 1
        query_vertices = []
        for j in range(k):
            query_vertices.append(int(data[index]))
            index += 1
        queries.append(query_vertices)
    
    # For each query, check if any candidate works
    results = []
    for query_vertices in queries:
        candidate_set = set()
        # Add all vertices in query
        for v in query_vertices:
            candidate_set.add(v)
            
        # Add parent of each query vertex
        for v in query_vertices:
            if v != 1:  # not root
                candidate_set.add(parent[v])
                
        # Add children of each query vertex
        for v in query_vertices:
            for child in children[v]:
                candidate_set.add(child)
        
        # Try each candidate in candidate_set
        found = False
        for u in candidate_set:
            # Build path from root to u (ancestors of u)
            path = set()
            curr = u
            while curr != 0:
                path.add(curr)
                curr = parent[curr]
            
            # Check if all query vertices are covered by path
            covered = True
            for v in query_vertices:
                # v is covered if v is in path or parent(v) is in path or any child of v is in path
                if v in path:
                    continue
                if parent[v] in path:
                    continue
                # Check if any child of v is in path
                child_in_path = False
                for child in children[v]:
                    if child in path:
                        child_in_path = True
                        break
                if not child_in_path:
                    covered = False
                    break
            
            if covered:
                found = True
                break
        
        results.append("YES" if found else "NO")
    
    # Output results
    for res in results:
        print(res)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

- So candidate_set = {3,8,9,10,1,7,9,10} = {1,3,7,8,9,10}

  - 8: parent=7 in path - good
  - 9: in path - good
  - 10: parent=9 in path - good
- So u=9 works -> "YES"

Example 2: [2,4,6]
- candidates: 2,4,6, parent of 2=1, parent of 4=1, parent of 6=2, children of 2=5,6; children of 4=none; children of 6=none
- candidate_set = {2,4,6,1,5}
- Try u=1: path={1}
  - 2: parent=1 in path - good
  - 4: parent=1 in path - good

- v=6: parent=2, children=5,6? Wait, from input: edge 2-6, so children of 2 has 6, and 6 has no children
- v=10: parent=9, children=none

- Try u=6: path={1,2,6}
  - 6: in path - good
  - 10: parent=9 not in path, children=none - not covered

  - 6: parent=2 not in path, children=none - not covered

It matches.

I think this solution is correct.

Now, about complexity: the total sum of k_i is 2*10^5, and for each query vertex, we add up to deg(v) children, and deg(v) is at most n.

In worst case, if a vertex has many children, and many queries contain it, the total work could be large.

But note that the sum of k_i is only 2*10^5, and each vertex in the tree might appear in multiple queries.

However, the total work over all queries in building the candidate_set is sum over all query vertices v of (1 + 1 + deg(v)) = sum over query vertices of (2 + deg(v)).

Since there are total k_i vertices across all queries, and each such vertex might have deg(v) children, worst-case total work is O(sum k_i * max_degree).

In a tree, max_degree can be O(n), and sum k_i = 2*10^5, so worst-case total work O(2*10^5 * n) = O(2*10^10) which might be too slow in Python.

We need to optimize.

But in the problem, it's guaranteed that the sum of k_i is only 2*10^5, and the tree has n<=2*10^5.

We might need a better approach.

However, in practice, trees are not complete, and average degree is small.

Or perhaps the intended solution is different.

But given that the constraints on sum k_i are small, and the query vertices are not necessarily covering high-degree vertices, it might pass.

To be safe, we can try to only add the parent and the vertex, and avoid adding children, because the children might not be necessary.

Let me reconsider: is it possible that for a query vertex v, we only need parent(v) or v to be on the path, and not any child of v?

In the example [3,8,9,10], we needed u=9 which has parent 7, and 8's parent is 7, 9's parent is 7.

In that case, for v=8, we had parent(8)=7, which is on the path when u=9.

For v=10, we had parent(10)=9, which is on the path when u=9.

In the path to u=9, we have [1,7,9], and 10 is covered because parent(10)=9 is on the path.

So we didn't need to consider children of any vertex.

Similarly, in other examples, we only needed parent(v) or v.

Is it possible that we only need v or parent(v) to be on the path?

Yes! Because if a child of v is on the path, then that child is on the path, and since the child is adjacent to v, v is covered.

But we don't need to consider the child of v as a candidate for being on the path; we only need that the child of v is on the path to cover v.

In the condition for covering v, if parent(v) is on the path, v is covered.

If v is on the path, v is covered.

If a child of v is on the path, then v is at distance 1 from that child, so v is covered.

But in terms of the path, if a child of v is on the path, that means it's an ancestor of u, so that child is on the path.

But to cover v, we don't need to add that child to the candidate set for u; we can just check if that child is on the path.

In the algorithm above, for a given u, we check if any child of v is on the path.

So we still need to know the children of v.

But we don't need to add every child of v to the candidate_set.

The candidate_set can be just the query vertices and their parents.

Then for each candidate u, we check if for every query vertex v, v is on path or parent(v) is on path or some child of v is on path.

But in the example [3,8,9,10], we need u such that 10 is covered.

If we only try query vertices and their parents: {3,8,9,10,1,7}
- u=7: path={1,3,7}
  - 3: in path - good
  - 8: parent=7 in path - good
  - 9: parent=7 in path - good
  - 10: parent=9 not in path, children of 10: none - not covered
- u=9: path={1,7,9}
  - 10: parent=9 in path - good
- So it works.

In the example with [6,10], we need to have parent(10)=9 in path for 10 to be covered.

So if we only try {6,10,2,9} (parents), then:
- u=9: path={1,7,9}
  - 6: parent=2 not in path, children of 6: none - not covered
- u=6: path={1,2,6}
  - 10: parent=9 not in path, children of 10: none - not covered
- So "NO"

So it still works.

Therefore, we can reduce the candidate_set to only {v, parent(v) for v in query}.

The size is at most 2*k for each query.

Total over all queries, sum of k_i is 2*10^5, so total candidates is O(sum k_i) = 2*10^5.

For each candidate, we build the path (O(depth) and then check for each query vertex whether it or its parent is on the path or if any child is on the path.

The checking for one query vertex in one candidate: O(deg(v)) for the children, which might be large.

But the total work over all query vertices and all candidates might be high.

We need to optimize the checking.

For a fixed candidate u, we have a fixed path.

For each query vertex v, we need to check if any child of v is in the path.

We can precompute for each v the children, and then for each v, check if any child is in the path.

The total work for one candidate is sum over query vertices v of (number of children of v).

In worst case, if a vertex has many children, and many queries contain it, it could be large.

However, the sum of k_i is only 2*10^5, and each vertex in the tree might have many children, but in total, the sum of degrees is 2*(n-1).

But we are summing over v in query vertices.

In worst case, if many queries contain a vertex with high degree, it could be bad.

But perhaps it's acceptable given the constraints.

Since the total sum of k_i is only 2*10^5, and each child is only added when v is in a query, the total work over all queries and all candidates for checking children might be acceptable.

In the worst case, if one query has many vertices with many children, but the sum of k_i is bounded, it might be acceptable.

I'll go with this optimized version.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def main():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    n = int(data[0])
    m = int(data[1])
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    index = 2
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[index])
        v = int(data[index+1])
        index += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # BFS to build parent and children arrays
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    children = defaultdict(list)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    parent[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                children[u].append(v)
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Process queries
    queries = []
    for i in range(m):
        k = int(data[index])
        index += 1
        query_vertices = []
        for j in range(k):
            query_vertices.append(int(data[index]))
            index += 1
        queries.append(query_vertices)
    
    # For each query, check if any candidate works
    results = []
    for query_vertices in queries:
        candidate_set = set()
        # Add all vertices in query
        for v in query_vertices:
            candidate_set.add(v)
            if v != 1:
                candidate_set.add(parent[v])
        
        # Try each candidate in candidate_set
        found = False
        for u in candidate_set:
            # Build path from root to u (ancestors of u)
            path = set()
            curr = u
            while curr != 0:
                path.add(curr)
                curr = parent[curr]
            
            # Check if all query vertices are covered by path
            covered = True
            for v in query_vertices:
                # v is covered if v is in path or parent(v) is in path or some child of v is in path
                if v in path:
                    continue
                if parent[v] in path:
                    continue
                # Check if any child of v is in path
                child_in_path = False
                for child in children[v]:
                    if child in path:
                        child_in_path = True
                        break
                if not child_in_path:
                    covered = False
                    break
            
            if covered:
                found = True
                break
        
        results.append("YES" if found else "NO")
    
    # Output results
    for res in results:
        print(res)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

This should work and is more efficient in terms of candidate set size.

The total number of candidates across all queries is O(sum k_i) = 2*10^5.

For each candidate and each query vertex, we do a constant work (in terms of the child checking, but the sum of child sizes might be large).

The worst-case total work might be high, but given the problem constraints and that the sum of k_i is only 2*10^5, it might pass in PyPy or in C++ but in Python it might be borderline.

But I think it's the best we can do.

  - 1: in path - good
  - 2: parent=1 in path - good
  - 5: parent=2 not in path, children of 5: none - not covered

  - 2: in path - good
  - 1: in path - good
  - 5: parent=2 in path - good
- So "YES"

  - 7: parent=3 not in path, children of 7=8,9,10 not in path - not covered
- Try u=3: path={1,3}
  - 5: parent=2 not in path - not covered
- Try u=4: path={1,4}
  - 5: parent=2 not in path - not covered
- Try u=5: path={1,2,5}
  - 4: parent=1 in path - good
  - 5: in path - good
  - 7: parent=3 not in path, children of 7=8,9,10 not in path - not covered
- Try u=7: path={1,3,7}
  - 5: parent=2 not in path - not covered
- Try u=8: path={1,7,8}
  - 5: parent=2 not in path - not covered
- Try u=9: path={1,7,9}
  - 5: parent=2 not in path - not covered
- Try u=10: path={1,9,10}
  - 5: parent=2 not in path - not covered
- So "NO"

It matches.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.


-----Example-----
Input
10 6
1 2
1 3
1 4
2 5
2 6
3 7
7 8
7 9
9 10
4 3 8 9 10
3 2 4 6
3 2 1 5
3 4 8 2
2 6 10
3 5 4 7

Output
YES
YES
YES
YES
NO
NO

The second query is $[2, 4, 6]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=2$. Then the vertex $4$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $6$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $2$ which belongs to this path.

The third query is $[2, 1, 5]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=5$ and all vertices of the query belong to this path.

The fourth query is $[4, 8, 2]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=9$ so vertices $2$ and $4$ both have distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which belongs to this path.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a rooted tree with n vertices, root is vertex 1
2. For each query with k vertices, we need to determine if there exists a path from root to some vertex u such that:
   - Each query vertex either lies on the path from root to u, OR
   - Each query vertex has distance 1 (is adjacent) to some vertex on the path

## Key Insights

1. The path from root to u is unique in a tree
2. For a vertex v to be "covered" by a path from root to u, either:
   - v is on the path, OR
   - v is adjacent to some vertex on the path

3. If a vertex v is to be covered by a path from root to u, then v must be on the path from root to u, or v must be adjacent to a vertex on that path.

4. Important observation: If a vertex v is adjacent to a vertex w on the path, then w must be a parent of v (since the path goes from root to u, and in a tree, adjacency only works in one direction).

5. For each query vertex, we can consider its parent. If a query vertex is not on the path, it must have a parent that lies on the path.

6. The path must contain either the query vertex itself or the parent of the query vertex.

## Algorithm

1. Build the tree and compute parent relationships (using DFS or BFS from root)
2. For each query:
   - For each query vertex v, we need either v to be on the path, or parent(v) to be on the path
   - The path from root to u must contain all the "required" vertices (either v or parent(v))
   - This means the path must contain all the vertices that are NOT already on a path from root to some ancestor

3. More specifically:
   - We need to find a vertex u such that for every query vertex v:
     - Either v is on the path from root to u, OR
     - parent(v) is on the path from root to u

4. The path from root to u is determined by the LCA (Lowest Common Ancestor) of all the required vertices.

5. Actually, a simpler approach:
   - For each query vertex v, it can be covered if either v is on the path, or parent(v) is on the path
   - So we need to find a path that contains all the "necessary" vertices, where necessary vertices are:
     - For each v in query, either v or parent(v)

6. The key insight is that if we consider all vertices that need to be covered, the path must contain either the vertex itself or its parent. 

Wait, let me reconsider. 

Actually, the path must contain at least one of each vertex or its parent. But since the path goes from root to u, it must contain all ancestors of u.

The path must contain all vertices from root to u. 

For each query vertex v, we need either:
- v is on the path (so v is an ancestor of u), OR
- parent(v) is on the path (so parent(v) is an ancestor of u)

In other words, for each query vertex v, either v or parent(v) must be in the path from root to u.

This means the path must contain at least one of v or parent(v) for each query vertex v.

The path from root to u is exactly the sequence of ancestors of u. 

Now, since we want to minimize constraints, we can think about which vertices must be included.

For each query vertex v:
- If v is not the root, we need either v or parent(v) in the path
- If v is the root, we already have it

The path must contain the LCA of all the "required" vertices, but we need to check if there exists a path that covers all constraints.

A simpler approach:
- For each vertex in the query, we get a constraint: it must be covered by either the vertex itself or its parent
- This means the path must contain either v or parent(v) for each v in the query
- The path from root to some u must contain at least one of these pairs
- The constraint is satisfied if and only if there exists some u such that for every query vertex v, either v is an ancestor of u or parent(v) is an ancestor of u

How to check this efficiently?

Actually, here's the key insight: if we consider the path from root to u, it contains vertices from root to u. 

For vertex v in query:
- v is on the path if v is in the path from root to u
- parent(v) is on the path if parent(v) is in the path from root to u

So v can be covered if v is in the path or parent(v) is in the path.

This means we need to find u such that for every v in query, v is in path or parent(v) is in path.

Since the path is from root to u, if we let P be the set of vertices on the path, then P must contain at least one of v or parent(v) for each v in query.

Now, notice that parent(v) is always on the path from root to u if v is on the path (since v is an ancestor of u, so parent(v) is also an ancestor).

The critical observation: if a vertex v is not on the path, its parent must be on the path.

So the path must contain either v or parent(v).

So we need a path that covers v or parent[v].

If we take parent[v], it's on all paths from root to descendants of parent[v].

If we take v, it's on paths to v and descendants.

So for one vertex, the answer is "YES" because either parent[v] is on the path (always, for any u in subtree of parent[v]), or we take v.

In fact, for any u in the subtree of parent[v], parent[v] is on the path. So if parent[v] is in the tree, we can always choose u such that parent[v] is on the path.

Therefore, for a single vertex, answer is always "YES".

For multiple vertices, we need to check.

I will implement the solution with candidate-based checking. 

Let me code accordingly. 

Note: We must ensure that when we compute ancestors of u, we only include ancestors, not the path to u.

We can do:

 def get_ancestors(u):
     ancestors = set()
     while u != 1:
         ancestors.add(u)
         u = parent[u]
     ancestors.add(1)
     return ancestors

This is O(depth) per call.

For each query, for each candidate in candidates, we do:

    ancestors_u = get_ancestors(u)
    for each v in query:
        if v in ancestors_u or parent[v] in ancestors_u:
            continue
        else:
            break
    else:
        return "YES"  # found a candidate

If no candidate works, return "NO"

We will do this for each query.

Total work: for each query, number of candidates * (k_i * depth)

With total sum of k_i = 2e5, and if the depth is small, it might be acceptable.

In worst-case, if the tree is a chain and one query has many vertices, it might be too slow, but perhaps the test cases are not worst-case.

Given the constraints, we hope it works.

Let me implement. 

We also need to build the tree and compute parent using BFS from root.

Steps:

1. Read n, m
2. Build adjacency list
3. Do BFS from root (1) to compute parent and depth
4. For each query:
   a. Read k and list of vertices
   b. Create a set of candidates: for each v in list, add v and parent[v]
   c. For each candidate u in candidates:
        - Get ancestors of u (set of vertices from u to root)
        - For each v in list:
            - if v in ancestors or parent[v] in ancestors: continue
            - else: break (this candidate doesn't work)
        - If we complete the loop, return "YES"
   d. If no candidate works, return "NO"

We must avoid duplicates in candidates, so use a set.

Let me code accordingly. 

We'll use a while loop to get ancestors.

Note: parent of 1 is 0 or None, so for v=1, parent[1] is 0, and should be ignored since not in tree.

In the query, v might be 1.

For v=1: in the query, we need either 1 on the path (which is always on the path) or parent[1] on the path. parent[1] is not in tree, so only 1 on path.

Since 1 is always on the path (root), for any v=1, it is covered.

So for any query that contains 1, it's always covered.

Therefore, we don't need to worry about parent[1].

In the candidate set, parent[1] should not be added (or we can avoid it).

In code, when v=1, parent[1] should be skipped or not added.

So in candidate creation, for each v in query:
   if v == 1:
       add only 1
   else:
       add v and parent[v]

But parent[v] might be 0, and we only want vertices from 1 to n.

So we can add only if parent[v] is between 1 and n.

Since parent[v] is stored, and in the tree, for any vertex v>1, parent[v] is in 1..n.

For v=1, parent[1] is not defined, so skip.

So in code:

 candidates = set()
 for v in query_vertices:
     if v == 1:
         candidates.add(1)
     else:
         candidates.add(v)
         candidates.add(parent[v])

Then for each candidate in candidates, we do the check.

Let me code. 

Note: the total sum of k_i is only 2e5, so even if we do O(k_i^2 * depth) per query, in practice the depth might be small or the number of candidates might be small.

In the worst-case, if the tree is a chain and a query has many vertices, then k_i is large, and the work per query might be large, but the total sum of k_i is 2e5, so the maximum k_i might be not too large.

For example, if we have 1000 queries each with 200 vertices, sum is 200000, which is acceptable.

In the work, per query, we do about 2*k_i * (k_i * depth) = 2 * k_i^2 * depth.

The sum over queries of k_i^2 might be large if k_i is large for many queries.

For example, if one query has k_i = 500, then work is 2*500^2 * depth = 500000 * depth, and if depth=2000, it's 1e9, which might be too slow in Python.

But the total sum of k_i is 2e5, so the maximum k_i might be bounded.

Let me write the code. 

We will use a BFS to build parent array.

Note: in the tree, vertices are 1-based.

We will create a set for each query's candidates.

We will not store the ancestors as a set for each u, because we will compute it for each candidate.

But we can avoid computing the ancestors set if we break early.

In the inner loop for v in query, we break as soon as one fails.

So we can do.

Final code:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    
    n = int(data[0])
    m = int(data[1])
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    index = 2
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[index])
        v = int(data[index+1])
        index += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # BFS to compute parent and depth (only for vertices 1 to n)
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    parent[1] = 0  # parent of root is 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Process queries
    results = []
    for _ in range(m):
        k = int(data[index])
        index += 1
        vertices = []
        for i in range(k):
            vertices.append(int(data[index]))
            index += 1
        
        # Build candidate set: for each vertex in query, add the vertex and its parent (if not 1, parent is defined; for 1, only 1)
        candidates = set()
        for v in vertices:
            if v == 1:
                candidates.add(1)
            else:
                candidates.add(v)
                candidates.add(parent[v])
        
        # Check each candidate
        found = False
        for u in candidates:
            if u < 1 or u > n:
                continue
                
            # Get ancestors of u (set of vertices from u to root)
            ancestors = set()
            curr = u
            while curr != 0 and curr != 1:  # when curr==0, parent is 0, so stop when we hit root
                ancestors.add(curr)
                curr = parent[curr]
            # Add root
            ancestors.add(1)
            
            # Check if all vertices in the query are covered by ancestors or their parent
            valid = True
            for v in vertices:
                # v is covered if v in ancestors or parent[v] in ancestors
                # parent[v] for v=1 is 0, so skip
                if v == 1:
                    # 1 is in ancestors (always, because we added 1)
                    continue
                if v in ancestors or parent[v] in ancestors:
                    continue
                else:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        if found:
            results.append("YES")
        else:
            results.append("NO")
    
    # Output results
    for res in results:
        print(res)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
```

Note: in the ancestors set, we add 1 at the end.

In the parent array, parent[1] = 0, and for any vertex v, parent[v] is stored.

In the loop, when we go from u to parent until we hit 0 or 1, we break when curr==0 or curr==1.

We only add vertices that are in 1..n.

When we add parent[curr], if parent[curr] is 0, we skip in the while loop.

In the while loop: we start from u, and we do while curr != 0 and curr != 1.

But parent[1] is 0, so when curr=1, we break.

So we only add vertices from u to the parent until we reach 1.

Actually, we want to add all ancestors from u to root.

We can do:

    ancestors = set()
    curr = u
    while curr != 1 and parent[curr] != 0: # but parent of 1 is 0
        ancestors.add(curr)
        curr = parent[curr]
    ancestors.add(1)

But if u=1, then we skip the while and add 1.

So we can do:

    ancestors = set()
    curr = u
    while curr != 1:
        ancestors.add(curr)
        curr = parent[curr]
    ancestors.add(1)

This is safe.

```python
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:

    visited = [False] * (n+1)

    visited[1] = True
    parent[1] = 0  # parent of root is 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                queue.append(v)

        index += 1

        for i in range(k):
            vertices.append(int(data[index]))
            index += 1

        candidates = set()

            if v == 1:
                candidates.add(1)
            else:
                candidates.add(v)
                candidates.add(parent[v])
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.


-----Example-----
Input
10 6
1 2
1 3
1 4
2 5
2 6
3 7
7 8
7 9
9 10
4 3 8 9 10
3 2 4 6
3 2 1 5
3 4 8 2
2 6 10
3 5 4 7

Output
YES
YES
YES
YES
NO
NO

Consider the queries.

The first query is $[3, 8, 9, 10]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path from the root $1$ to the vertex $u=10$. Then vertices $[3, 9, 10]$ belong to the path from $1$ to $10$ and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which also belongs to this path.

The second query is $[2, 4, 6]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=2$. Then the vertex $4$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $6$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $2$ which belongs to this path.

The third query is $[2, 1, 5]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=5$ and all vertices of the query belong to this path.

The fourth query is $[4, 8, 2]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=9$ so vertices $2$ and $4$ both have distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which belongs to this path.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a rooted tree with root at vertex 1. For each query with a set of vertices, we need to determine if there exists a path from root (vertex 1) to some vertex u such that:
- Each vertex in the query either:
  1. Belongs to the path from root to u, OR
  2. Has distance exactly 1 to some vertex on this path

In other words, every query vertex must be either on the path or adjacent to a vertex on the path.

## Key Insights

1. The path from root to any vertex u is unique and goes through ancestors of u.

2. For a vertex v to be "covered" by a path from root to u:
   - v must be on the path (v is an ancestor of u), OR
   - v must be a neighbor of some vertex on the path (v is adjacent to an ancestor of u)

3. A vertex v can only be adjacent to an ancestor of u if v is a direct neighbor of some ancestor of u.

4. For a given path from root to u, the set of vertices covered by this path consists of:
   - All ancestors of u (including u itself)
   - All vertices that are adjacent to ancestors of u

5. For each query, we need to check if there exists some vertex u such that the query vertices are all covered by the path from root to u.

## Algorithm Approach

1. First, build the tree and compute parent pointers and depths for all vertices using DFS/BFS from root.

2. For each query, we need to check if there's a valid path endpoint u.

3. Key observation: The path must cover all query vertices. If a query vertex v is not on the path from root to u, then v must be adjacent to some vertex on the path.

4. For each query vertex, we can determine what constraints it imposes:
   - If v is not on the path, then v must be adjacent to some ancestor of u.
   - This means u must be such that some ancestor of u is adjacent to v.

5. A crucial insight: The path from root to u must extend far enough to cover the "deepest" requirement. 

6. For each query vertex v:
   - If v is not on the path, then some ancestor of u must be adjacent to v.
   - The ancestors of u that can be adjacent to v are limited to the parents of v or v itself (but v itself is only adjacent to its parent).

Steps:
1. Read input, build tree, compute parent and depth arrays with BFS/DFS from root 1.
2. For each query:
   a. Read the vertices in the query.
   b. Build a set of candidates: initially all vertices in query and all parents of vertices in query.
   c. Also add the root (1) to candidates.
   d. For each candidate u in candidates:
       - Check if for every v in query, either v is an ancestor of u or parent(v) is an ancestor of u.
       - If yes, answer "YES" and break.
   e. If no candidate works, answer "NO".

To avoid duplicates in candidates, use a set.

Note: depth[u] is defined as the distance from root to u.

We'll implement is_ancestor(v, u) as follows:
- If depth[v] > depth[u]: return False
- While depth[u] > depth[v]: u = parent[u]
- return u == v

But we can do without depth by moving u up until depth[u] <= depth[v], then check if equals.

Actually, since we have parent array, we can move u up until depth[u] <= depth[v].

But we don't store depth. We need to store depth.

We'll compute depth in the tree building.

Algorithm for depth: during BFS from root, depth[1]=0, then for each neighbor, depth[child] = depth[parent] + 1.

Let me code accordingly.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    # Parse first two numbers
    n = int(data[0])
    m = int(data[1])
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    index = 2
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[index])
        v = int(data[index+1])
        index += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Compute parent and depth arrays with BFS from root 1
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Function to check if v is an ancestor of u
    def is_ancestor(v, u):
        if depth[v] > depth[u]:
            return False
        # Move u up until its depth is at most depth[v]
        while depth[u] > depth[v]:
            u = parent[u]
        return u == v
    
    # Process queries
    results = []
    for _ in range(m):
        k = int(data[index])
        index += 1
        vertices = []
        for _ in range(k):
            vertices.append(int(data[index]))
            index += 1
        
        # Build candidate set: vertices in query and their parents
        candidates = set()
        for v in vertices:
            candidates.add(v)
            if parent[v] != 0:  # v is not root
                candidates.add(parent[v])
        candidates.add(1)  # root
        
        found = False
        for u in candidates:
            # Check if for every vertex in query, either it is on path to u or its parent is on path to u
            valid = True
            for v in vertices:
                if not (is_ancestor(v, u) or is_ancestor(parent[v], u)):
                    valid = False
                    break
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        results.append("YES" if found else "NO")
    
    # Output results
    for res in results:
        print(res)

solve()
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    
    n = int(data[0])
    m = int(data[1])
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    index = 2
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[index])
        v = int(data[index+1])
        index += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Compute parent and depth arrays with BFS from root 1
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Function to check if v is an ancestor of u
    def is_ancestor(v, u):
        if depth[v] > depth[u]:
            return False
        # Move u up to depth[v] or below
        while depth[u] > depth[v]:
            u = parent[u]
        return u == v
    
    # Process queries
    results = []
    for _ in range(m):
        k = int(data[index])
        index += 1
        vertices = []
        for _ in range(k):
            vertices.append(int(data[index]))
            index += 1
        
        # Build candidate set: vertices in query and their parents
        candidates = set()
        for v in vertices:
            candidates.add(v)
            if parent[v] != 0:  # v is not root
                candidates.add(parent[v])
        candidates.add(1)  # root
        
        found = False
        for u in candidates:
            valid = True
            for v in vertices:
                if not (is_ancestor(v, u) or is_ancestor(parent[v], u)):
                    valid = False
                    break
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        results.append("YES" if found else "NO")
    
    # Output results
    for res in results:
        print(res)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

View Extracted Code 
from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    
    n = int(data[0])
    m = int(data[1])
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    index = 2
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u = int(data[index])
        v = int(data[index+1])
        index += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Compute parent and depth arrays with BFS from root 1
    parent = [0] * (n+1)
    depth = [0] * (n+1)
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Function to check if v is an ancestor of u
    def is_ancestor(v, u):
        if depth[v] > depth[u]:
            return False
        # Move u up to depth[v] or below
        while depth[u] > depth[v]:
            u = parent[u]
        return u == v
    
    # Process queries
    results = []
    for _ in range(m):
        k = int(data[index])
        index += 1
        vertices = []
        for _ in range(k):
            vertices.append(int(data[index]))
            index += 1
        
        # Build candidate set: vertices in query and their parents
        candidates = set()
        for v in vertices:
            candidates.add(v)
            if parent[v] != 0:  # v is not root
                candidates.add(parent[v])
        candidates.add(1)  # root
        
        found = False
        for u in candidates:
            valid = True
            for v in vertices:
                if not (is_ancestor(v, u) or is_ancestor(parent[v], u)):
                    valid = False
                    break
            if valid:
                found = True
                break
        
        results.append("YES" if found else "NO")
    
    # Output results
    for res in results:
        print(res)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rooted tree consisting of $n$ vertices numbered from $1$ to $n$. The root of the tree is a vertex number $1$.

A tree is a connected undirected graph with $n-1$ edges.

You are given $m$ queries. The $i$-th query consists of the set of $k_i$ distinct vertices $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$. Your task is to say if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers $n$ and $m$ ($2 \le n \le 2 \cdot 10^5$, $1 \le m \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of vertices in the tree and the number of queries.

Each of the next $n-1$ lines describes an edge of the tree. Edge $i$ is denoted by two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$, the labels of vertices it connects $(1 \le u_i, v_i \le n, u_i \ne v_i$).

It is guaranteed that the given edges form a tree.

The next $m$ lines describe queries. The $i$-th line describes the $i$-th query and starts with the integer $k_i$ ($1 \le k_i \le n$) — the number of vertices in the current query. Then $k_i$ integers follow: $v_i[1], v_i[2], \dots, v_i[k_i]$ ($1 \le v_i[j] \le n$), where $v_i[j]$ is the $j$-th vertex of the $i$-th query.

It is guaranteed that all vertices in a single query are distinct.

It is guaranteed that the sum of $k_i$ does not exceed $2 \cdot 10^5$ ($\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m} k_i \le 2 \cdot 10^5$).


-----Output-----

For each query, print the answer — "YES", if there is a path from the root to some vertex $u$ such that each of the given $k$ vertices is either belongs to this path or has the distance $1$ to some vertex of this path and "NO" otherwise.


-----Example-----

9 10

3 2 4 6
3 2 1 5
3 4 8 2
2 6 10
3 5 4 7

Output
YES
YES
YES
YES
NO
NO

The second query is $[2, 4, 6]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=2$. Then the vertex $4$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $6$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $2$ which belongs to this path.

The third query is $[2, 1, 5]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=5$ and all vertices of the query belong to this path.

The fourth query is $[4, 8, 2]$. The answer is "YES" as you can choose the path to the vertex $u=9$ so vertices $2$ and $4$ both have distance $1$ to the vertex $1$ which belongs to this path and the vertex $8$ has distance $1$ to the vertex $7$ which belongs to this path.
